Morse Index Stability of Branched Willmore Immersions
Alexis Michelat

Abstract
We show the Morse index stability for sequences of Willmore immersions of bounded energy without assuming that the limiting immersion and the bubbles are free of branch points.



Mathematical subject classification : 35J35, 35J48, 35R01, 49Q10, 53A05, 53A10, 53A30, 53C42.

1
Introduction

1.1
Overture

Geometers, as Chern predicted it in a famous interview, can no longer restrict themselves to the smooth category. In this article, we address the second analytic step in the long-standing research program initiated by Tristan Rivière a decade ago (
[
22
]
) to solve Kusner’s conjecture—that consists in finding the optimal sphere eversion. After Smale proved in
1957
that the path of immersions from the round sphere
S^{2}
into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is path-connected (
[
23
]
), geometers struggled to find a path of immersions that connects the standard immersion of the round sphere
\iota:S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
to the antipodal embedding
-\iota
. Examples were eventually constructed by several mathematicians, but were involved and it was unclear if one could make them simpler. In
1983
, Robert Bryant classified the Willmore spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and showed that they are all inversions of minimal surfaces with embedded planar ends (
[
6
]
)—this result was partially extended to various settings (
[
10
,
17
]
) before Dorian Martino finally extended it to all branched immersions a couple of years ago (
[
13
]
). The relevance of this result lies in two facts: the Willmore energy can be seen as a distance function from the immersion of the round sphere and it should be possible to compute the Morse index (for the Willmore energy) of inversions of minimal surfaces. The second point was actually not considered until T. Rivière shared his vision with at the end of
2015
. It should not come as a surprise to anyone aware of the important recent developments of geometric analysis that once more, this vision was correct (
[
14
,
16
]
; see also
[
8
,
7
]
). The first fact led however Robert Kusner (
[
9
]
) to imagine that one may flow the Willmore energy from a given Willmore surface to the round sphere. The idea is to find a surface that would have the suitable geometric properties to be also flowed to the antipodal embedding of the sphere. Amongst the surfaces classified by R. Bryant, the first non-trivial family has energy
16\pi
.

Although the approach using gradient flows did not bear fruits in this direction so far (see however
[
20
,
21
]
), T. Rivière designed an alternative approach using min-max methods. Before describing it, let us introduce the Willmore energy by an usual approach that draws ideas from particle physics. It makes the Willmore energy proceed from an alternative to the problem of Plateau, that consists in finding a surface of prescribed boundary that minimises the area. This problem caught the attention of countless mathematicians and constitutes the third important development in the calculus of variations after the proof of the existence of a solution to the Dirichlet’s problem by Hilbert in 1900 and the proof of the existence of a non-trivial closed geodesic on any simply connected domain by Birkhoff in 1917. Assuming the reader familiar with the rudiments of Sobolev spaces, we recall that for any domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(where
d\geq 1
), the Dirichlet energy
E:W^{1,2}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by:

\displaystyle E(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

Independently solved in 1930 by Radó and in 1931 by Douglas for curves in Euclidean space, the problem of Plateau can also be formulated in Riemannian manifold and treated by similar methods. The most obvious generalisation is to look at model spaces, namely the round sphere equipped with its induced metric from its embedding into Euclidean space and in the hyperbolic space. In the latter example, we place ourselves in the half-space model
\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{+}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
equipped with the metric

\displaystyle g_{hyp}=\frac{dx^{2}+dy^{2}+dz^{2}}{z^{2}}

of constant sectional curvature
-1
. The problem of Plateau in this setting can be formulated as follows: for any Jordan curve
\Gamma\subset\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\left\{0\right\}
(here, this notation of asymptotic boundary means that we take the boundary of the space for a conformal change of metric that makes its smooth up to the boundary), does there exist a surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
that locally minimises the area and such that
\partial_{\infty}\Sigma=\Gamma
? Anderson (
[
3
]
) proved the existence of a solution to this problem for any rectifiable curve, and a solution happens to be a current of finite mass as a subset of this Euclidean space. However, due to the singularity of the metric at infinity, its area is infinite. This is where an important idea of physics—renormalisation—comes into play. The idea, that in this specific context can be attributed to Graham-–Witten (
[
24
]
), consists in computing the area of

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\varepsilon}=\Sigma\cap\left\{(x,y,z):z>\varepsilon\right\}

and to remove the singularity terms in
\varepsilon
to get a finite quantity. Although the final result depends on the choice of the defining function and the limit has no reason to be finite, it was identified by Alexakis–-Mazzeo (
[
1
]
; see also
[
2
]
) who proved the following result.

Theorem 1.1

(Alexakis–Mazzeo)
.

Let
0<\alpha<1
and
\Gamma\subset\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a
C^{3,\alpha}
curve and
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a minimal surface such that
\partial_{\infty}\Sigma=\Gamma
. Then, the renormalised area defined by

\displaystyle\mathscr{RA}(\Sigma)=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\left(\mathrm{Area}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})-\frac{\mathscr{L}(\partial\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}\right)

is finite and the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\mathscr{RA}(\Sigma)=-2\pi\chi(\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\mathring{A}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{\Sigma},

where
\chi(\Sigma)
is the Euler characteristic and
\mathring{A}
is the trace-free second fundamental form of
\Sigma
.

Now, we will adopt a parametric approach and fix a Riemann surface
\Sigma
and an immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
. The quantity

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}(\vec{\Phi})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\mathring{A}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
g=\vec{\Phi}^{*}g_{\mathrm{hyp}}
is the induced metric on
\Sigma
, is known as the Willmore energy. For immersions into Euclidean space, the Willmore energy is usually defined as follows:

\displaystyle W(\vec{\Phi})=\int_{\Sigma}H^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}

is the mean curvature.
Thanks to Gauss-Bonnet theorem, both functionals only differ by a topological constant and are therefore analytically equivalent. A property first established by Blaschke and Thomsen in 1923 (to be precise, one can find it already in the Doktorarbeit of Thomsen in 1922, and a certain W. Schadow—whose entire existence is shrouded with mystery—is the conformal invariance of the Willmore energy. In other words, it is invariant by ambient translations, rotations, dilations, and inversions. More precisely, the quantity
|\mathring{A}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}
is a pointwise conformally invariant quantity, which implies that inversions of complete minimal surfaces—for which the mean curvature
H
vanishes identically—with finite total curvature are compact Willmore surfaces. However, in general, those surfaces may have branch points, where the map
\vec{\Phi}
can be expanded in a conformally chart as a holomorphic map as follows

\displaystyle\vec{\Phi}(z)=\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{A}_{0}z^{m}\right)+o(|z|^{m}),

where
m\geq 2
is an integer. Those are the immersions that we consider in this article, or more precisely, we place ourselves in the setting of
[
18
]
but allow the limiting immersions and the bubble to develop branch points.

1.2
Technical Aspects

As in
[
18
]
, an important first step in the proof is to show that negative variations cannot be located in neck regions. This is accomplished by using the Rellich and Hardy–Rellich inequalities already established in
[
18
, Chapter 2]
, and more refined estimates using the properties of the operators:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}=|x|^{1-m}\Delta(|x|^{m-1}(\,\cdot\,))=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}},

where
m\geq 1
is the
integer
multiplicity of the branch point.
Using the decomposition of the forth-order elliptic differential operator with regular singularities
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}
as the non-trivial sum of two positive operators and integrating by parts the weighted
L^{2}
norm of
\mathscr{L}_{m}u
, it is rather straightforward to show that for all
\alpha>0
, there exists a universal constant
C_{\alpha}<\infty
such that for all
0<a<b<\infty
, defining
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\alpha}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\alpha}\right)dx\leq C_{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

From this inequality and the refined estimates established for the second fundamental form of Willmore immersions in neck regions, it is easy to show that negative variations cannot be located in the neck regions. However, we face another difficulty that is far more challenging than in the unbranched case
m=1
when we try to establish that eigenvalues for the weighted Willmore operator are bounded from below. As in
[
18
]
, the proof is based on a rather involved weighted Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality. In the case
m=1
, by a standard decomposition of any function
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega)
between a function that vanishes on the boundary and a function that solves a Dirichlet problem, the estimate followed from establishing the inequality for biharmonic functions. The obvious generalisation is to consider solutions of
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u=0
, but if the required estimate does indeed hold, it does not suffice for our purpose. Indeed, in the next argument using the Bochner identity, due to the non-flatness of the conical metric in neck regions, comparing the Bochner identity for the flat metric on which a weight is applied
a posteriori
(this is the expression furnished by our Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality), in order to estimate one of the extra terms, it is necessary to have a weight on the right-hand side of the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality. However, such an estimate is
neither
verified for functions in
W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)

nor
for solutions of
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u=0
(due to the loss of the logarithmic singularity once integrated with respect to a weight). The situation may seem hopeless but the remedy is to notice that the correct generalisation of biharmonic functions in our context is not solutions of the partial differential equation
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u=0
but (real) solutions of

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{m}u=16\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\overline{\mathfrak{L}_{m}}^{\ast}\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right)=0,

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{m}=|z|^{1-m}\partial_{z}^{2}\left(|z|^{m-1}(\,\cdot\,)\right)=\partial_{z}^{2}+\frac{(m-1)}{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{4z^{2}}.

Notice that both operators both have leading term given by the bi-Laplacian and have regular singularities. Otherwise, their properties radically differ, although it may not be obvious to see on their expanded expressions:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{m}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}-\frac{6(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{2}}\Delta+4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{8(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla

\displaystyle+\frac{(m+1)(m-1)^{2}(m-3)}{|x|^{4}},

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta^{2}+\frac{2(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}.

In other words, instead of seeing the biharmonic energy as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u\right)^{2}dx=16\int_{\Omega}\left(\partial_{z\overline{z}}^{2}u\right)^{2}dx,

we see it as

\displaystyle 16\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|dz|^{2}.

and by an immediate integration by parts, both functionals coincide on
W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
. From the analytical viewpoint, the advantage of working with
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
(seen as an operator acting on
real-valued
functions) has a four-dimensional Kernel (while the Kernel of
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}
is infinite-dimensional—is it equal to the space of harmonic functions weighted by
|x|^{1-m}
). This explains why the doubly weighted Rellich and Hardy–Rellich inequalities for
\mathfrak{L}_{m}
do hold true on
W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, as long as we assume that
m\geq 3
). This restriction on
m
may seem problematic, but in codimension
1
, Laurain–Rivière showed that the branch point must be odd due to a connexity argument on the first homotopy group of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
(
[
11
]
). Although it seems reductive, for the previous Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality, we need to assume

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle>\frac{2}{3}+\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{-1-\frac{2^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\sqrt[3]{32+3\sqrt{114}}}+\sqrt[3]{2(32+3\sqrt{114})}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-\frac{8}{9}+\frac{2^{\frac{8}{3}}}{9\sqrt[3]{32+3\sqrt{114}}}-\frac{4}{9}\sqrt[3]{2(32+3\sqrt{114})}+\frac{88}{9\sqrt{-1-\frac{2^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\sqrt[3]{32+3\sqrt{114}}}+\sqrt[3]{2(32+3\sqrt{114})}}}}

\displaystyle=2.039423\cdots

and this condition seems unavoidable. Furthermore, the radial terms can be estimated without having to use the delicate argument involving a refinement of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality that was necessary for the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality for solutions of
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u=0
due to the logarithm components. However, the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality for solutions of
\mathfrak{D}_{m}u=0
is excessively more technical for the Taylor expansion mostly (that is, for all except the first two Fourier frequencies) involves transcendent functions that have no primitive expressible with respect to standard functions (and the roots are quite involved too), which makes it especially difficult to estimate the
“crossed terms”
of the Fourier coefficients in the various
L^{2}
norms. This forces us to use the refined Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for most frequencies, but the exact technical details would be tedious to explain here so let us simply state the useful lemma that involves the Cauchy–Schwarz identity that is instrumental in the proof.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measured space and
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(X,\mu,\mathbb{C})
. Then, for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\lambda_{1}||\lambda_{2}|}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}(x)&f_{2}(x)\\
f_{1}(y)&f_{2}(y)\end{pmatrix}\right|^{2}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\leq\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(X)}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(X)}\int_{X}\left|\lambda_{1}\,f_{1}+\lambda_{2}\,f_{2}\right|^{2}d\mu.

1.3
Main Theorem

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
n\geq 3
. Then, there exists a universal constant
0<\Lambda_{n}<\infty
with the following property. Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface and
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathrm{Imm}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a sequence of Willmore immersions of bounded energy:

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})<\infty.

Assume that
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
bubble-converges to
(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{1},\cdots,\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{m},\vec{\Psi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\Psi}_{p},\vec{\chi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\chi}_{q})
, where all Willmore immersions and assume that
either

n=3
,
or
that no limiting Willmore immersion has branched points of order
m=2
and let
\left\{\gamma_{k}^{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}^{N}\right\}\subset\Sigma
be the set of shrinking geodesics of
(\Sigma,g_{k})
. There exists a universal constant
\Lambda_{3}>0
such that the bound

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\max_{1\leq l\leq N}\frac{|\vec{\gamma}_{1}(\vec{\Phi}_{k},\gamma_{k}^{l})|}{\mathscr{L}(\gamma_{k}^{l})}\leq\Lambda_{n}

(1.1)

implies that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\mathrm{Ind}_{W}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})+\mathrm{Null}_{W}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})\right)\leq\sum_{l=1}^{m}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}^{0}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{l})+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}^{0}(\vec{\Psi}_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}^{0}(\vec{\chi}_{j})<\infty

(1.2)

where
\mathrm{Ind}^{0}_{W}=\mathrm{Ind}_{W}+\mathrm{Null}_{W}
.

For the definition of bubble-convergence, see
[
5
]
and
[
18
, Definition 1.1.6]
. Notice that the quantization and bubble-convergence always hold thanks to the combined work of Bernard–Rivière
[
5
]
and Laurain–Rivière
[
11
]
(in the latter case, this is because our condition (
1.1
) implies the condition of Laurain–Rivière for the quantization of energy).

1.4
Finale

What would be the next step in Rivière’s program? It consists in generalising the present article to the case of branched Willmore spheres obtained by min-max (
[
22
]
). The first difficulty is to control the non-local terms arising in the second derivative. Otherwise, the main obstacle is to understand how Rellich-type estimates can show that the contribution coming from the viscosity term is negligible. Here, the analogy with harmonic maps from Da Lio–Gianocca–Rivière’s theory (
[
12
]
) breaks down for min-max need not work for Sacks–Uhlenbeck-type estimates. However, the Willmore energy is more flexible and there are no topological obstructions for the upper semi-continuity of the Morse index plus nullity.

2
Inequalities for the Classical Family of Weighted Inequalities

In this technical section, we establish two fundamental estimates that play a crucial role in the proof. They are by no means immediate, and when it comes to the second inequality, it does not follow from a mere application of the methods first developed in
[
18
]
and we have to come up with several refinements of the proof.

2.1
Weighted Inequalities Associated to a Family of Fourth-Order Elliptic Operators

Compare to
[
18
]
, for
m>1
, the proof is significantly more technical, but also holds for a larger range of exponents.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
m>1
and define

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}.

For all
\alpha>0
, there exist a universal constant
C_{m,\alpha}<\infty
with the following property.
Fix
0<a<b<\infty
and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
. Then, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}\right)dx&&\leq C_{m,\alpha}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx\\
&\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}\right)dx&&\leq C_{m,\alpha}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1)

Proof.

Recall that (see
[
18
, (2.2.5) p.97]
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta^{2}+2(m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}.

(2.2)

Furthermore, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\mathscr{L}_{m}u)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+(m^{2}-1)\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx.

(2.3)

This identity is based on the following decomposition

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m,1}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m,1}+4(m^{2}-1)\mathscr{D}_{2}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{2}=\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m},

(2.4)

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m,1}=\Delta+\frac{(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathscr{D}_{2}=\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla-\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}=\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}

Let us check the identity (
2.4
). Since
\mathscr{L}_{m,1}
is a self-adjoint operator, we have

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\mathscr{L}_{m,1}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+(m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m^{2}-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla(\,\cdot\,)+\frac{4(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{4}}

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m,1}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m,1}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+2(m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m^{2}-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla(\,\cdot\,)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}+4(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{4}}.

(2.5)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\mathscr{D}_{2}=\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}+\frac{2}{r^{3}},

which shows that

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{2}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}-\frac{2}{r^{4}}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}-\frac{1}{r^{4}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla(\,\cdot\,)-\frac{1}{|x|^{4}}

(2.6)

and we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m,1}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m,1}+4(m^{2}-1)\mathscr{D}_{2}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{2}=\Delta^{2}+2(m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m^{2}-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla(\,\cdot\,)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}+4(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{4}}

\displaystyle-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+4(m^{2}-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla(\,\cdot\,)-\frac{4(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{4}}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+2(m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}},

which allows us to recover (
2.2
), and provides a new proof of (
2.3
).

Part 1: Radial estimates.

Let
\alpha>0
. Thanks to (
2.3
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx\leq\frac{1}{4(m^{2}-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

Furthermore, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, notice that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\dive\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}u^{2}\right)|x|^{\alpha-2}dx=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}u^{2}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\left(|x|^{\alpha}\right)\right)dx

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha-2}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.7)

where we used that
\nabla\log|x|=\dfrac{x}{|x|^{2}}
and the harmonicity of
\log
in dimension
2
. Therefore, we have by (
2.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx-2\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx+\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx+(\alpha-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.8)

Therefore, for all
\alpha\geq 1
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4(m^{2}-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

(2.9)

Likewise, for all
\alpha>1
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{1}{4(\alpha-1)(m^{2}-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

(2.10)

Now, notice that (
2.1
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality show that for all
\alpha\neq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx=\frac{-2}{\alpha-2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{2}{|\alpha-2|}\left(\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore, we deduce that for all
\alpha\neq 2

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{4}{(\alpha-2)^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.11)

Therefore, if
\alpha<2
, we get by (
2.1
) and (
2.11
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx+(\alpha-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{4(1-\alpha)}{(\alpha-2)^{2}}\right)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(\alpha-2)^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.12)

Therefore, for all
0<\alpha<2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{(2-\alpha)^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx,

(2.13)

which shows that for all
0<\alpha<2
, we have by (
2.11
) and (
2.13
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

(2.14)

Furthermore, since the estimate also holds for negative
\alpha
, we also get for all
\alpha>0

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{(2+\alpha)^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx

(2.15)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

(2.16)

Therefore, we need only establish the tangential estimate.

Part 2: Tangential estimates.

Interestingly enough, in the most technical lemma of the article, this is the radial estimate that is the most delicate, while here, the tangential estimate is slightly more difficult to establish. For all
u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
and
v\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}(uv)

\displaystyle=\left(\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)(uv)=v\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)

\displaystyle+2\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla v+u\left(\Delta v+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v\right)

\displaystyle=v\,\mathscr{L}_{m}u+2\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla v+u\left(\Delta v+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v\right).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)v\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}v\,dx+2\int_{\Omega}\left(\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\right)\mathscr{L}_{m}u\,dx+\int_{\Omega}u\,\mathscr{L}_{m}u\left(\Delta v+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v\right)dx.

(2.17)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m,1}\left(uv\right)=\left(\Delta+\frac{m^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}}\right)(uv)=v\mathscr{L}_{m,1}u+2\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla v+u\,\Delta v,

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{2}(uv)=\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla-\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\right)\left(uv\right)=v\,\mathscr{D}_{2}u+u\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v\right).

Using the identity (
2.4
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)\,v\,dx=\int_{\Omega}u\left(\mathscr{L}_{m,1}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m,1}u\right)v\,dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}u\left(\mathscr{D}_{2}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{2}u\right)v\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m,1}u\right)^{2}v\,dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{D}_{2}u\right)^{2}v\,dx+2\int_{\Omega}\left(\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\right)\mathscr{L}_{m,1}u\,dx+\int_{\Omega}\left(u\,\mathscr{L}_{m,1}u\right)\Delta v\,dx

\displaystyle+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}u\,\mathscr{D}_{2}u\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v\right)dx.

(2.18)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}-\mathscr{L}_{m,1}

\displaystyle=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}-\left(\Delta+\frac{m^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}}\right)=2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla-\frac{2(m-1)}{|x|^{2}}

\displaystyle=2(m-1)\mathscr{D}_{2}.

(2.19)

Comparing (
2.17
) and (
2.1
), we deduce by (
2.1
) that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}v\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m,1}u\right)^{2}v\,dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{D}_{2}u\right)^{2}v\,dx-4(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\right)\mathscr{D}_{2}u\,dx

\displaystyle-2(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(u\,\mathscr{D}_{2}u\right)\Delta v\,dx-2(m-1)\int_{\Omega}u\,\mathscr{L}_{m}u\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v\right)dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}u\,\mathscr{D}_{2}u\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v\right)dx.

(2.20)

Now, if we take
v(x)=|x|^{\alpha}
(where
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
), we get

\displaystyle\nabla v=\alpha\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}|x|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\Delta v=\left(\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}\right)r^{\alpha}=\alpha^{2}|x|^{\alpha-2}.

Therefore, the identity (
2.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m,1}u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}\,dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{D}_{2}u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle-4\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx-2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}u\,\mathscr{L}_{m}u\,|x|^{\alpha-2}dx

\displaystyle+\left(4\alpha(m^{2}-1)-2\alpha^{2}(m-1)\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.21)

Using (
2.1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle-4\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx=-4\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle-2\alpha(\alpha-2)(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.22)

Then, we have

\displaystyle-2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}u\,\Delta u\,|x|^{\alpha-2}dx=2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}|x|^{\alpha}dx+2\alpha(\alpha-2)(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle=2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}|x|^{\alpha}dx-\alpha(\alpha-2)^{2}(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.23)

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle-2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}u\,\mathscr{L}_{m}u\,|x|^{\alpha-2}dx=-2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}u\,\Delta u\,|x|^{\alpha-2}dx-4\alpha(m-1)^{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle-2\alpha(m-1)^{3}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle=2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}|x|^{\alpha}dx+\left(-\alpha(\alpha-2)^{2}(m-1)+2\alpha(\alpha-2)(m-1)^{2}-2\alpha(m-1)^{3}\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.24)

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx=-\frac{(\alpha-2)}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx-\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx=-\frac{\alpha}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx

(2.25)

and since
4\alpha(m^{2}-1)-2\alpha^{2}(m-1)=2\alpha(m-1)(2(m+1)-\alpha)
, we get

\displaystyle\left(4\alpha(m^{2}-1)-2\alpha^{2}(m-1)\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx=-\alpha^{2}(m-1)(2(m+1)-\alpha)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.26)

Gathering (
2.1
), (
2.1
), (
2.1
), (
2.1
), and (
2.26
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+\frac{m^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle-2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx+2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{\left|\partial_{\theta}u\right|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle+\Big(-2\alpha(\alpha-2)(m-1)-\alpha(\alpha-2)^{2}(m-1)+2\alpha(\alpha-2)(m-1)^{2}-2\alpha(m-1)^{3}

\displaystyle-\alpha^{2}(m-1)(2(m+1)-\alpha)\Big)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+\frac{m^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle-2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx+2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{\left|\partial_{\theta}u\right|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx-2\alpha(m-1)\Big(m^{2}-1+\alpha\Big)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

Therefore, we can compactly rewrite the identity as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+\frac{m^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle-2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}|x|^{\alpha}dx+2\alpha(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\frac{\left|\partial_{\theta}u\right|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx-2\alpha(m-1)\Big(m^{2}-1+\alpha\Big)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx

(2.27)

Therefore, if
\alpha>0
, we get by (
2.1
), (
2.10
), (
2.13
), and (
2.14
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{\theta}u|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha(m-1)}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx+\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle+(m^{2}-1+\alpha)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha(m-1)}+\frac{1}{4(m^{2}-1)}+\frac{m^{2}-1+\alpha}{4(\alpha-1)(m^{2}-1)}\right)\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,\alpha>1\\
&\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha(m-1)}+\frac{(2-\alpha)^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}+\frac{m^{2}-1+\alpha}{\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}\right)\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,0<\alpha<2,\end{aligned}\right.

(2.28)

while we get by (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) (recall (
2.3
) too) for that for
\alpha>0

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{\theta}u|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha(m-1)}\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+\frac{m^{2}-1}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}dx\right.

\displaystyle\left.+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}dx\right)+\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)^{2}\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle+(m^{2}-1-\alpha)_{+}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\alpha}dx

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha(m-1)}+\frac{(2+\alpha)^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}(m^{2}-1)}\right)\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx

(2.29)

and this concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

We need to strengthen this estimate by adding weights on the right-hand side, but the cost is to have to add a term corresponding to the
L^{2}
norm of
\partial_{z}^{2}u
once we make a change of variable.

2.2
Refined Weighted Inequalities

If we want to add a weight in the right-hand side of the inequality, we need to replace
\mathscr{L}_{m}
by the operator
\mathfrak{L}_{m}
that correspond to
|z|^{1-m}\partial_{z}^{2}
after a change of variation
v=|z|^{m-1}u
.

Theorem 2.2
.

For all
3\leq m<\infty
, define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{m}=|z|^{1-m}\partial_{z}^{2}(|z|^{m-1}(\,\cdot\,))=\partial_{z}^{2}+\frac{m-1}{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{4z^{2}}.

Fix
0<a<b<\infty
and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
. Then, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have for all
-\infty<\beta<\dfrac{m-1}{4}

(
such that
\beta\neq 1/2
)
and for all
-\infty<\gamma<\dfrac{m-1}{2}

(
such that
\gamma\neq 1
)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{4\beta}dx\leq\frac{8}{(m-1)(m-1-4\beta)(1-2\beta)^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|x|^{4\beta}dx\\
&\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}|x|^{2\gamma}\leq\frac{8}{(m-1)(m-1-2\gamma)}\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|x|^{2\gamma}dx.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.30)

Proof.

First,
the identity (
2.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right)|x|^{\alpha}dx=-\frac{(\alpha-2)}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.31)

It implies that for all
\alpha\neq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{4}{(\alpha-2)^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}|x|^{\alpha}dx.

(2.32)

Now, we compute for all
\alpha\neq 0

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\mathscr{L}_{m}u|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}dx=\int_{\Omega}\left|\partial_{z}^{2}u+\frac{m-1}{z}\partial_{z}u+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{4z^{2}}u\right||z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+(m-1)^{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}(m-3)^{2}}{16}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+2(m-1)\mathrm{Re}\,\int_{\Omega}\partial_{z}^{2}u\,\partial_{\overline{z}}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}}|dz|^{2}+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{2}\mathrm{Re}\,\int_{\Omega}u\,\partial_{z}^{2}u\,\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}^{2}}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(m-1)^{2}(m-3)}{2}\mathrm{Re}\,\int_{\Omega}\frac{\partial_{z}u}{z}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}^{2}}|dz|^{2}.

(2.33)

We have the elementary computation
\partial_{z}|z|^{\alpha}=\partial_{z}(z\overline{z})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}=\dfrac{\alpha}{2}\overline{z}|z|^{\alpha-2}
, which yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\partial_{z}^{2}u\,\partial_{\overline{z}}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\partial_{z}u\,\partial_{z\overline{z}}^{2}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}}|dz|^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{z}u\cdot\partial_{\overline{z}}u|z|^{\alpha-2}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(\partial_{z}u\right)^{2}\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}}|dz|^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\alpha-2)}{4}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\,\partial_{z}u\right)^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2},

(2.34)

where we used that

\displaystyle\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}}\right)=-\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}^{2}}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\frac{z}{\overline{z}}|z|^{\alpha-2}=\frac{(\alpha-2)}{2}z^{2}|z|^{\alpha-4},

which can also be found by writing

\displaystyle\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}}\right)=\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(z|z|^{\alpha-2}\right)=\frac{(\alpha-2)}{2}z^{2}|z|^{\alpha-4}.

Next, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u\,\partial_{z}^{2}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}^{2}}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\partial_{z}u\right)^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\int_{\Omega}u\,\partial_{z}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha-2}}{\overline{z}}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\partial_{z}u\right)^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{4}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{z}\left(u^{2}\right)\frac{|z|^{\alpha-2}}{\overline{z}}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\partial_{z}u\right)^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+\frac{\alpha(\alpha-2)}{8}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}.

(2.35)

Finally, we also have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{\partial_{z}u}{z}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha}}{\overline{z}^{2}}|dz|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}u\,\partial_{z}u\frac{|z|^{\alpha-2}}{\overline{z}}|dz|^{2}=-\frac{(\alpha-2)}{4}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}.

(2.36)

Gathering (
2.2
), (
2.2
), (
2.2
), and (
2.36
), we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+(m-1)^{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}(m-3)^{2}}{16}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(m-1)(\alpha-2)}{2}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{Re}\,\left\{\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\partial_{z}u\right)^{2}\right\}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}-(m-1)\alpha\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{2}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{Re}\,\left\{\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\partial_{z}u\right)^{2}\right\}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)\alpha(\alpha-2)}{8}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{(m-1)^{2}(m-3)(\alpha-2)}{8}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}.

(2.37)

Using the elementary inequality

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Re}\,\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\partial_{z}u\right)|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}\right|\leq\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2},

we deduce that for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}\geq\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left((m-1)^{2}+\frac{(m-1)(\alpha-2)}{2}-\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{2}-(m-1)\alpha\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{(m-1)^{2}(m-3)^{2}}{16}+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)\alpha(\alpha-2)}{8}-\frac{(m-1)^{2}(m-3)(\alpha-2)}{8}\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(m-1)\left(m-1-\alpha\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{16}\left(2\alpha^{2}-2(m+1)\alpha+(m^{2}-1)\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|z|^{4}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}.

(2.38)

The discriminant of the polynomial
2\,X^{2}-2(m+1)X+(m^{2}-1)
is equal to

\displaystyle D=-4(m^{2}-2m-3)=-4(m+1)(m-3)\leq 0,

which shows that for all
m\geq 3
and for all
\alpha<m-1
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}|x|^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{8}{(m-1)(m-1-\alpha)}\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2},

and for all
\alpha\neq 2
and
\alpha<m-1
, the inequality (
2.32
) shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{32}{(m-1)(m-1-\alpha)(\alpha-2)^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}.

Notice that if
m>3
, for all
\alpha\leq m-1
, we get the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}|x|^{\alpha}dx\leq\frac{16}{(m-1)(m-3)(2\alpha^{2}-2(m+1)\alpha+(m^{2}-1))}\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}.

Now, if
1<m<3
, we have

\displaystyle 2\alpha^{2}-2(m+1)\alpha+(m^{2}-1)=2\left(\alpha-\frac{(m+1)-\sqrt{(m+1)(3-m)}}{2}\right)\left(\alpha-\frac{(m+1)+\sqrt{(m+1)(3-m)}}{2}\right),

which shows that

\displaystyle\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{16}\left(2\alpha^{2}-2(m+1)\alpha+(m^{2}-1)\right)\left(2\alpha^{2}-2(m+1)\alpha+(m^{2}-1)\right)\geq 0

if and only if

\displaystyle\frac{(m+1)-\sqrt{(m+1)(3-m)}}{2}\leq\alpha\leq\frac{(m+1)+\sqrt{(m+1)(3-m)}}{2}.

Since

\displaystyle\frac{(m+1)-\sqrt{(m+1)(3-m)}}{2}=\frac{m^{2}-1}{m+1+\sqrt{(m+1)(3-m)}}=\alpha_{m}\in]0,2[,

we have to find a new argument for
\alpha<\alpha_{m}
. Therefore, assuming that
\alpha<\alpha_{m}
, we get thanks to (
2.32
) and (
2.2
) (notice also that
|\nabla u|^{2}=4|\partial_{z}u|^{2}
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|\mathfrak{L}_{m}u\right|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}\geq\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(m-1)\left(m-1-\alpha\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{(\alpha-2)^{2}}\left(2\alpha^{2}-2(m+1)\alpha+(m^{2}-1)\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(m-1)}{2(\alpha-2)^{2}}\left(-\alpha^{3}+(5m-9)\alpha^{2}+4(m^{2}-m-3)\alpha+2(m^{3}-3m^{2}+m+1)\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\partial_{z}u|^{2}}{|z|^{2}}|z|^{\alpha}|dz|^{2}.

We see that in general, we will only get an estimate for a restricted range of
\alpha
, but we are mostly interested to prove an estimate for
|\alpha|
small. Furthermore, since we are mostly interested in the case
m=2
, we now assume that
m=2
, which yields

\displaystyle-\alpha^{3}+(5m-9)\alpha^{2}+4(m^{2}-m-3)\alpha+2(m^{3}-3m^{2}+m+1)=-\alpha^{3}+\alpha^{2}+4\alpha-2.

However, the roots of
Q=-X^{3}+X^{2}+4\,X-2
are all real and given by

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}-\frac{13(1+i\sqrt{3})}{6\sqrt[3]{-8+3i\sqrt{237}}}-\frac{1}{6}(1-i\sqrt{3})\sqrt[3]{-8+3i\sqrt{237}}=-1.81360\cdots

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}-\frac{13(1-i\sqrt{3})}{6\sqrt[3]{-8+3i\sqrt{237}}}-\frac{1}{6}(1+i\sqrt{3})\sqrt[3]{-8+3i\sqrt{237}}=0.47068\cdots

\displaystyle r_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}+\frac{13}{3\sqrt[3]{-8+3i\sqrt{237}}}+\frac{1}{3}\sqrt[3]{-8+3i\sqrt{237}}=2.34429\cdots

In particular, the quantity is negative for
r_{1}<\alpha<r_{2}
, and we cannot obtain the expected estimate for negative
\alpha
. However, the estimate holds true for
r_{2}<\alpha<r_{3}
, and it holds in particular if
\alpha=2\gamma
with
\frac{1}{4}\leq\gamma<1
. However, for
\alpha<0
, even if we use (
2.1
), we will not be able to get the needed estimate since the tangential part of the gradient has the wrong sign. The breakdown of estimates for
m=2
is an issue that will reappear in the next estimates.
∎

2.3
Weighted Gagliardo–Nirenberg-type Inequality

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
P=3\,X^{4}-8\,X^{3}+10\,X^{2}-16\,X+7\in\mathbb{R}[X]
and let

\displaystyle m_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}+\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{-1-\frac{2^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\sqrt[3]{32+3\sqrt{114}}}+\sqrt[3]{2(32+3\sqrt{114})}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-\frac{8}{9}+\frac{2^{\frac{8}{3}}}{9\sqrt[3]{32+3\sqrt{114}}}-\frac{4}{9}\sqrt[3]{2(32+3\sqrt{114})}+\frac{88}{9\sqrt{-1-\frac{2^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\sqrt[3]{32+3\sqrt{114}}}+\sqrt[3]{2(32+3\sqrt{114})}}}}

\displaystyle=2.039423\cdots

be its largest real root. Fix
m_{0}<m<\infty
, and let

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{m}=\partial_{z}^{2}+\frac{m-1}{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{4z^{2}}.

For all
0<\beta<1/2
and
0<\gamma<1
, there exists
\Lambda_{\gamma}<\infty
such that for all
0<a<b<\infty
, assuming that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\Lambda_{\gamma},

the following property is verified.
Define
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For all function
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega)
such that
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u=0
, there exists a universal constant
C_{m,\beta,\gamma}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\left|\nabla u+(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}u\right|^{2}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)dx\leq C_{m,\beta,\gamma}\left(\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{4\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{4\beta}\right)dx\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega}\left|\partial_{z}^{2}u+\frac{m-1}{z}\partial_{z}u+\frac{(m-1)(m-3)}{4z^{2}}u\right|^{2}|dz|^{2}\right).

(2.39)

The proof, as the one of the corresponding inequality for
m=1
proved in
[
18
]
, simply consists in showing that the
L^{2}
norm of a
m
-biharmonic function (a function such that
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u=0
) controls the
l^{2}
norm of its Fourier coefficients. In fact, not all coefficients of the radial part of
u
will be directly estimable, so we will have to use another argument. Since it is the most technical part of the proof, we give the fundamental technical lemmas separately. Since the lack of direct estimate comes from the insufficiency of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we first compute the missing term in the inequality.

2.3.1
Refinements of the Cauchy-Schwarz Inequality

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measured space. Then, for all
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(X,\mu,\mathbb{C})
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)-\left|\int_{X}f_{1}\overline{f_{2}}\,d\mu\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(y)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y).

(2.40)

Proof.

Indeed, Fubini’s theorem implies that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)=\int_{X\times X}|f_{1}(x)|^{2}|f_{2}(y)|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y).

(2.41)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{X}f_{1}\overline{f_{2}}d\mu\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{X}f_{1}(x)\overline{f_{2}(x)}d\mu(x)\right)\left(\int_{X}\overline{f_{1}(y)}f_{2}(y)d\mu(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{X\times X}f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)\overline{f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\int_{X\times X}f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)\overline{f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{X\times X}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)\overline{f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)}\right)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

(2.42)

Furthermore, we have by (
2.41
) and symmetry

\displaystyle\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left(|f_{1}(x)|^{2}|f_{2}(y)|^{2}+|f_{1}(y)|^{2}|f_{2}(x)|^{2}\right)d\mu(x)d\mu(y),

(2.43)

and finally, we have by (
2.3.1
) and (
2.43
)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)-\left|\int_{X}f_{1}\overline{f_{2}}\,d\mu\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left(|f_{1}(x)|^{2}|f_{2}(y)|^{2}+|f_{1}(y)|^{2}|f_{2}(x)|^{2}-2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)\overline{f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)}\right)\right)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y),

(2.44)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measured space. For all
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(X,\mu,\mathbb{C})
, for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}|}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}(x)&f_{2}(x)\\
f_{1}(y)&f_{2}(y)\end{pmatrix}\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)\leq\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(X)}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(X)}\int_{X}\left|\lambda_{1}\,f_{1}+\lambda_{2}\,f_{2}\right|^{2}d\mu.

(2.45)

Proof.

For all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we estimate thanks to estimate
2.40
of Lemma
2.4

\displaystyle\int_{X}\left|f_{1}+\lambda\,f_{2}\right|^{2}d\mu=\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu+\lambda^{2}\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu+2\lambda\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\int_{X}f_{1}\overline{f_{2}}d\mu\right)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu+\lambda^{2}\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu-2|\lambda|\left|\int_{X}f_{1}\overline{f_{2}}d\mu\right|

\displaystyle=\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu+\lambda^{2}\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu

\displaystyle-2|\lambda|\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu-2|\lambda|\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)}+\lambda^{2}\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu

\displaystyle+2|\lambda|\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)}

\displaystyle-2|\lambda|\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)}

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu}-|\lambda|\sqrt{\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{|\lambda|\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)}{\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)}+\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)}}.

Now, replacing
f_{1}
by
\lambda_{1}f_{1}
and
\lambda
by
\lambda_{2}
, we have (provided that
\lambda_{1}\neq 0
, but the estimate is trivial if
\lambda_{1}
or
\lambda_{2}
vanishes)

\displaystyle\int_{X}|\lambda_{1}\,f_{1}+\lambda_{2}\,f_{2}|^{2}d\mu=\lambda_{1}^{2}\int_{X}\left|f_{1}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}f_{2}\right|^{2}d\mu

\displaystyle\geq\left(|\lambda_{1}|\sqrt{\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu}-|\lambda_{2}|\sqrt{\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{|\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}|\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)}{\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)}+\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)}}.

(2.46)

In particular, we get the inequality

\displaystyle\frac{|\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}|}{2}\frac{\int_{X\times X}\left|f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)-f_{1}(y)f_{2}(x)\right|^{2}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)}{\sqrt{\left(\int_{X}|f_{1}|^{2}d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}|f_{2}|^{2}d\mu\right)}}\leq\int_{X}\left|\lambda_{1}\,f_{1}+\lambda_{2}\,f_{2}\right|^{2}d\mu,

(2.47)

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
0<a<b\leq e^{-1}
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
. Define
f_{1}(r)=r^{\gamma-\frac{1}{2}}\log(r)
and
f_{2}(r)=r^{\gamma-\frac{1}{2}}
. For all
\lambda>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda\,\frac{b^{2\gamma}}{\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)}\leq\frac{8\gamma^{2}(1+\gamma)}{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2}-4\gamma^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{a}^{b}\left(f_{1}+\lambda\,f_{2}\right)^{2}dr.

(2.48)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f_{1}^{2}(r)dr=\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}(r)dr=\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma}r^{2\gamma}\log^{2}(r)\right]_{a}^{b}-\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}\log(r)dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(b^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)-a^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\right)+\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}r^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)\right]_{a}^{b}+\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(b^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)-a^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\left(b^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)-a^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}b^{2\gamma}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right).

Then, we have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f_{1}(r)f_{2}(r)dr=\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}\log(r)dr=-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(b^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)-a^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}}b^{2\gamma}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right),

and finally

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f_{2}^{2}(r)=\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}dr=\frac{1}{2\gamma}b^{2\gamma}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right).

We readily see that the triangle does not give us any non-trivial estimate. Therefore, we need the refinenment of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality proven above.
Now, we get

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{b}\left(f_{1}(r)f_{2}(s)-f_{1}(s)f_{2}(r)\right)^{2}drds=\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}s^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)dr\,ds.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)ds=s^{2\gamma}\int_{\frac{a}{s}}^{\frac{b}{s}}t^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}(t)dt=s^{2\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma}t^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(t\right)-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}t^{2\gamma}\log\left(t\right)+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}t^{2\gamma}\right]_{\frac{a}{s}}^{\frac{b}{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(b^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)-a^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{s}{a}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\left(b^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)-a^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{s}{a}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}b^{2\gamma}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}s^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)dr\,ds

\displaystyle=\mathrm{(I)}+\mathrm{(II)}+\mathrm{(III)}+\mathrm{(IV)}+\mathrm{(V)}.

(2.49)

We first have

\displaystyle\mathrm{(I)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}b^{2\gamma}\int_{a}^{b}s^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)ds=\frac{1}{2\gamma}b^{4\gamma}\int_{\frac{a}{b}}^{1}t^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}(t)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}b^{4\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma}t^{2\gamma}\log^{2}(t)-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\log(t)+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}t^{2\gamma}\right]_{\frac{a}{b}}^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}b^{4\gamma}\left\{\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=b^{4\gamma}\left\{\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\}.

(2.50)

Then, we compute

\displaystyle\mathrm{(II)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\gamma}a^{2\gamma}\int_{a}^{b}s^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}\left(\frac{s}{a}\right)ds=-\frac{1}{2\gamma}a^{4\gamma}\int_{1}^{\frac{b}{a}}t^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}(t)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\gamma}a^{4\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma}t^{2\gamma}\log^{2}(t)-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}t^{2\gamma}\log(t)+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}r^{2\gamma}\right]_{1}^{\frac{b}{a}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\gamma}a^{4\gamma}\left\{\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}\left(\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2\gamma}-1\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=b^{4\gamma}\left\{-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)-\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right\}.

(2.51)

Likewise, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{(III)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}b^{2\gamma}\int_{a}^{b}s^{2\gamma-1}\log\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)ds=\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}b^{4\gamma}\int_{\frac{a}{b}}^{1}t^{2\gamma-1}\log(t)dt=\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}b^{4\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma}t^{2\gamma}\log(t)-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}}t^{2\gamma}\right]_{\frac{a}{b}}^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}b^{4\gamma}\left\{\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=b^{4\gamma}\left\{-\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2\gamma}+\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\}.

(2.52)

Likewise, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{(IV)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}a^{2\gamma}\int_{a}^{b}s^{2\gamma-1}\log\left(\frac{s}{a}\right)ds=-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}a^{4\gamma}\int_{1}^{\frac{b}{a}}t^{2\gamma-1}\log(t)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}a^{4\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma}t^{2\gamma}\log(t)-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}}t^{2\gamma}\right]_{1}^{\frac{b}{a}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}a^{4\gamma}\left\{\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2\gamma}-1\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=b^{4\gamma}\left\{-\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)+\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right\}.

(2.53)

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{(V)}=\frac{1}{4\gamma^{3}}b^{2\gamma}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)\int_{a}^{b}s^{2\gamma-1}ds=\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}b^{4\gamma}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2}.

(2.54)

Finally, gathering the estimates (
2.49
), (
2.3.1
), (
2.3.1
), (
2.3.1
), (
2.3.1
), and (
2.54
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{b}r^{2\gamma-1}s^{2\gamma-1}\log^{2}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)dr\,ds

\displaystyle=b^{4\gamma}\left\{\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}b^{4\gamma}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}b^{4\gamma}\left\{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2}-4\gamma^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\},

(2.55)

which notably furnishes the elementary inequality

\displaystyle 2\gamma\,x^{\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)<1-x^{2\gamma}\quad\text{for all}\;\gamma>0,\;\,\text{for all}\;0<x<1

(2.56)

which can also be checked directly. Now, we have the elementary estimate

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f_{1}^{2}(r)dr\leq\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(1+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\right)b^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{b}\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\int_{a}^{b}f_{1}^{2}(r)dr\int_{a}^{b}f_{2}^{2}(r)dr}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(1+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\right)b^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)\times\frac{1}{2\gamma}b^{2\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\gamma}\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}}b^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{a}^{b}\int_{a}^{b}\big(f_{1}(r)f_{2}(s)-f_{1}(s)f_{2}(r)\big)^{2}}{\sqrt{\int_{a}^{b}f_{1}^{2}(r)dr\int_{a}^{b}f_{2}^{2}(r)dr}}\geq\frac{1}{8\gamma^{4}}b^{4\gamma}\left\{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2}-4\gamma^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{2\sqrt{2}\gamma^{2}}{\sqrt{2\gamma^{2}+2\gamma+1}}\frac{1}{b^{2\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\gamma^{2}\sqrt{2\gamma^{2}+2\gamma+1}}\frac{b^{2\gamma}}{\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)}\left\{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2}-4\gamma^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\}.

Finally, using inequality (
2.40
) from Lemma
2.4
, we deduce that for all
\lambda>0

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{4\sqrt{2}\gamma^{2}\sqrt{2\gamma^{2}+2\gamma+1}}\frac{b^{2\gamma}}{\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)}\left\{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\right)^{2}-4\gamma^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\gamma}\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right\}\leq\int_{a}^{b}\left(f_{1}+\lambda\,f_{2}\right)^{2}dr,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

2.3.2
Proof of the Main Inequality

Proof.

Step 1.
Decomposition in Fourier modes.
Thanks to the appendix of
[
18
]
, the inequality is verified for
m=1
. We can therefore assume without loss of generality that
m>1
.
Expand
u
as

\displaystyle u(r,\theta)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{n}(r)e^{in\theta}.

Recall that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta^{2}+2(m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}.

We also have

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Delta

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{3}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\Delta

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{4}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{6}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{2}\Delta+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}\Delta+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\Delta

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{4}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{6}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{4}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{4}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{4}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{4}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{4}

\displaystyle+2(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\right)-4(m^{2}-1)\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{r^{4}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{2m^{2}-1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2m^{2}-1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{r^{4}}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{2(m^{2}+1)}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{4}.

Notice that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}=\partial_{r}^{2}+(2m-1)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}u.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{n^{2}}(\mathscr{L}_{m})=\partial_{r}^{2}+(2m-1)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}-n^{2}}{r^{2}}.

If
u=Y(\log(r))
, then

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} u^{\prime}&=\frac{1}{r}Y^{\prime}\\
u^{\prime\prime}&=\frac{1}{r^{2}}(Y^{\prime\prime}-Y^{\prime}),\end{aligned}\right.

and we get

\displaystyle\Pi_{n^{2}}(\mathscr{L}_{m})u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(Y^{\prime\prime}-Y^{\prime}+(2m-1)Y^{\prime}+(m-1-n)(m-1+n)Y\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(Y^{\prime\prime}+2(m-1)Y^{\prime}+(m-1-n)(m-1+n)Y\right).

Its characteristic polynomial is given by

\displaystyle P_{m}=X^{2}+2(m-1)X+(m-1)^{2}-n^{2},

whose discriminant is given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}=4(m-1)^{2}-4((m-1)^{2}-n^{2})=4n^{2},

which shows that the roots of
P_{m}
are given by
1-m\pm n
. Therefore, if
n\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\left(\Pi_{n^{2}}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}\right)\right)=\mathrm{Span}(r^{1-m+n},r^{1-m-n}).

For
n=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\left(\Pi_{0}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}\right)\right)=\mathrm{Span}\left(r^{1-m},r^{1-m}\log(r)\right).

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}=\Delta-2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}=\partial_{r}^{2}-(2m-3)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}.

We deduce that

\displaystyle\Pi_{n^{2}}(\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast})=\partial_{r}^{2}-(2m-3)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}-n^{2}}{r^{2}}.

Making the same change of variable
u=Y(\log(r))
, we get

\displaystyle\Pi_{n^{2}}(\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast})u=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(Y^{\prime\prime}-2(m-1)Y^{\prime}+(m-1-n)(m-1+n)Y\right),

which shows that (this result can also be inferred from
[
18
, p. 118]
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\left(\Pi_{n^{2}}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\right)\right)=\mathrm{Span}(r^{1+m+n},r^{1+m-n}).

For
n=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\left(\Pi_{0}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\right)\right)=\mathrm{Span}\left(r^{1+m},r^{1+m}\log(r)\right).

Therefore, if
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u=0
in
\Omega=\vec{B}_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, we deduce that there exists
\left\{a_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\left\{b_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle u(z)

\displaystyle=|z|^{1-m}\left(\alpha_{1}\log|z|+2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}z^{n}\right)\right)+|z|^{m+1}\left(\alpha_{2}\log|z|+2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{n}z^{n}\right)\right)

(2.57)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}\,r^{1-m}\log(r)+2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\,r^{1-m+n}e^{in\theta}\right)+\alpha_{2}r^{m+1}\log(r)+2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{n}\,r^{m+1+n}e^{in\theta}\right).

Step 2.
Estimations of the
L^{2}
norm of
\mathscr{L}_{m}u
.

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(r^{\alpha}\log(r)\right)=\alpha\,r^{\alpha-1}\log(r)+r^{\alpha-1}

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\left(r^{\alpha}\log(r)\right)=\alpha(\alpha-1)r^{\alpha-2}\log(r)+(2\alpha-1)r^{\alpha-2}

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}\left(r^{\alpha}\log(r)\right)=\left(\partial_{r}^{2}+(2m-1)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)\left(r^{\alpha}\log(r)\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\alpha-1)r^{\alpha-2}\log(r)+(2\alpha-1)r^{\alpha-2}+(2m-1)\alpha\,r^{\alpha-2}\log(r)+(2m-1)r^{\alpha-2}

\displaystyle+(m-1)^{2}r^{\alpha-2}\log(r)

\displaystyle=\left((m-1)^{2}+\alpha\left(2(m-1)+\alpha\right)\right)r^{\alpha-2}\log(r)+2(m-1+\alpha)r^{\alpha-2}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}\left(r^{m+1}\log(r)\right)

\displaystyle=\left((m-1)^{2}+(m+1)\left(2(m-1)+m+1\right)\right)r^{m-1}\log(r)+2(2m-1)r^{m-1}

\displaystyle=4m^{2}\,r^{m-1}\log(r)+4mr^{m-1}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}\left(r^{m+1+n}e^{in\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{r}^{2}+(2m-1)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\right)\left(r^{m+1+n}e^{in\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\left((m+n+1)(m+n)+(2m-1)(m+n+1)+(m-1)^{2}-n^{2}\right)r^{m+n-1}e^{in\theta}

\displaystyle=4m(m+n)r^{m+n-1}e^{in\theta}.

Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}u

\displaystyle=4m\left(m\alpha_{2}r^{m-1}\log(r)+\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)r^{m-1}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\left((m+n)b_{n}r^{m+n-1}+(m-n)\overline{b_{-n}}r^{m-n-1}\right)e^{in\theta}\right).

Therefore, if
\mathrm{Rad}
stands for the radial projection, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Rad}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}=16m^{2}\left(m^{2}\alpha_{2}^{2}r^{2m-2}\log^{2}(r)+\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)^{2}r^{2m-2}+2m\alpha_{2}\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)r^{2m-2}\log(r)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\left|(m+n)b_{n}r^{m+n-1}+(m-n)\overline{b_{-n}}r^{m-n-1}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=16m^{2}\left(m^{2}\alpha_{2}^{2}r^{2m-2}\log^{2}(r)+\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)^{2}r^{2m-2}+2m\alpha_{2}\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)r^{2m-2}\log(r)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}(m+n)^{2}|b_{n}|^{2}r^{2m+2n-2}+4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(m^{2}-n^{2}\right)\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}b_{-n}\right)r^{2m-2}\right).

Then, we have for
\alpha\neq-1

\displaystyle\int r^{\alpha}\log(r)dr

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\log(r)-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}\int r^{\alpha}dr=\frac{r^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\log(r)-\frac{1}{(\alpha+1)^{2}}r^{\alpha+1}

\displaystyle\int r^{\alpha}\log^{2}(r)dr

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\log^{2}(r)-\frac{2}{\alpha+1}\int r^{\alpha}\log(r)dr

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\log^{2}(r)-\frac{2}{(\alpha+1)^{2}}r^{\alpha+1}\log(r)+\frac{2}{(\alpha+1)^{3}}r^{\alpha+1},

while for all
\beta>0

\displaystyle\int\frac{\log^{\beta}(r)}{r}dr=\frac{1}{\beta+1}\log^{\beta+1}(r).

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx=32\pi\,m^{2}\int_{a}^{b}\left(m^{2}\alpha_{2}^{2}r^{2m-1}\log^{2}(r)+\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)^{2}r^{2m-1}+2m\alpha_{2}\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)r^{2m-1}\log(r)\right.

\displaystyle\left.2\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}(m+n)^{2}|b_{n}|^{2}r^{2m+2n-1}+4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(m^{2}-n^{2}\right)\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}b_{-n}\right)r^{2m-1}\right)dr

\displaystyle=32\pi\,m^{2}\bigg\{\frac{m}{2}\alpha_{2}^{2}\left(b^{2m}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)+\frac{1}{m}b^{2m}\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)+\frac{1}{2m^{2}}b^{2m}-\left(a^{2m}\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)+\frac{1}{m}a^{2m}\log\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)+\frac{1}{2m^{2}}a^{2m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2m}\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)^{2}b^{2m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)-\alpha_{2}\left(\alpha_{2}+2mb_{0}\right)\left(b^{2m}\log\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)+\frac{1}{2m}b^{2m}-\left(a^{2m}\log\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)+\frac{1}{2m}a^{2m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=1-m}^{\infty}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n-m)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{m}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(m^{2}-n^{2})\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}b_{-n}\right)b^{2m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)\bigg\}.

(2.58)

Step 3.
Estimation of the frequencies for
|n|\geq m+1
.

First, for all
n\geq m+1
, we have by Cauchy’s inequality
2ab\leq\varepsilon\,a^{2}+\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}b^{2}
(for
\varepsilon=\dfrac{1}{2}
)

\displaystyle m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+m^{2}(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n-m)}\right)

\displaystyle+2m(m+n)(m-n)\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}{b_{-n}}\right)b^{2m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2(n+m)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)

\displaystyle+m^{2}(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\left(\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n-m)}\right)-2b^{2(n-m)}\frac{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2(n+m)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{2}(n-m)}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+m)}}|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\left(\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n-m)}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+m)}\right)-2\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n-m)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2(n+m)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+\frac{m^{2}(n-m)}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+m)}}|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\left(1-4\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right).

Therefore, assuming that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\log\left(\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}\right),

we deduce that

\displaystyle m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+m^{2}(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n-m)}\right)

\displaystyle+2m(m+n)(m-n)\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}{b_{-n}}\right)b^{2m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2(n+m)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+\frac{1}{4}m^{2}(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}m^{2}\left((m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(n+m)}+(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\right).

(2.59)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle 8\pi\,m^{2}\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}+8\pi\,m^{2}\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}\leq\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

(2.60)

Step 4.
Estimation of the frequencies for
1\leq|n|\leq m
.

Notice that the crossed terms vanish for
m=n
, so we get for free

\displaystyle 64\pi\,m^{3}|b_{m}|^{2}b^{4m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{4m}\right)+8\pi\,m^{2}\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}+8\pi\,m^{2}\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}(n-m)|b_{-n}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n-m)}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx.

(2.61)

Now, for
1\leq n\leq m-1
, we have

\displaystyle m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+m^{2}(m-n)|b_{-n}|^{2}b^{2(m-n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right)

\displaystyle+2m(m+n)(m-n)\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}{b_{-n}}\right)b^{2m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)

\displaystyle\geq m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)-(m+n)^{2}(m-n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\frac{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}}b^{2(m+n)}\left(m^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right)-\left(m^{2}-n^{2}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}}\left(n^{2}-2m^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right).

Therefore, assuming that

\displaystyle 2m^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{2},

or

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{1}{m-n}\log\left(\frac{2m}{n}\right),

(2.62)

we deduce that

\displaystyle m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+m^{2}(m-n)|b_{-n}|^{2}b^{2(m-n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right)

\displaystyle+2m(m+n)(m-n)\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}{b_{-n}}\right)b^{2m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}(m+n)n^{2}|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}.

Likewise, we have

\displaystyle m^{2}(m+n)|b_{n}|^{2}b^{2(m+n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)+m^{2}(m-n)|b_{-n}|^{2}b^{2(m-n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right)

\displaystyle+2m(m+n)(m-n)\mathrm{Re}\,\left(b_{n}{b_{-n}}\right)b^{2m}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)

\displaystyle\geq m^{2}(m-n)|b_{-n}|^{2}b^{2(m-n)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right)-(m+n)(m-n)^{2}|b_{-n}|^{2}b^{2(m-n)}\frac{\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(m-n)|b_{-n}|^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}}b^{2(m-n)}\left(m^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}\right)-\left(m^{2}-n^{2}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2m}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(m-n)|b_{-n}|^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m+n)}}b^{2(m-n)}\left(n^{2}-2m^{2}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(m-n)}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}n^{2}(m-n)|b_{-n}|^{2}b^{2(m-n)}

thanks to the previous condition (
2.62
) on the conformal class. Gathering both estimates, we finally deduce by (
2.3.2
) that
